   o NOT TRANSCRIBED. The sub-records are unnamed and nothing reads
     the timers yet. [open]

9.33.  THE GEARBOX - and racer+0x88 is finally explained

   The bike moved but did not accelerate like retail, and the one
   place src/ knowingly ran invented logic was the gearbox:
   physics_gear_for_speed picked a gear from the ratios and snapped to
   it instantly. The real thing is a three-function state machine.

9.33.1.  A shift is requested, waited out, then applied

       maybe_Gearbox_RequestUpshift    0x0048e0a0 (was FUN_0048e0a0)
       maybe_Gearbox_RequestDownshift  0x0048e120 (was FUN_0048e120)
       maybe_Gearbox_TickShift         0x0048e6e0

   A requester queues a gear and arms a timer; the tick counts the
   timer down by the frame's milliseconds and only moves the gear when
   it expires. THE DELAY IS THE CLUTCH, and it is 150 to 300 ms - far
   longer than a frame, so it is plainly visible.

   o pending (racer+0x8c) IS SIGNED - positive counts gears up,
     negative counts them down - which is how several downshifts
     queue into one change. A second downshift request while one is
     already counting adds a gear WITHOUT restarting the clock.

   o AT THE TOP GEAR THE UPSHIFT REQUEST IS NOT A NO-OP. It writes
     gear = (int)nbSpeed. That matters because the protection
     subtracts 1.0 from nbSpeed on a failed check
     (copy-protection.txt), so a cracked copy is silently forced DOWN
     a gear at the moment it tries to reach top.

9.33.2.  racer+0x88 selects the gearbox character

   The field 9.20.2 found blocking the 1.1x torque multiplier, and
   which 9.20.3 could only say was NOT a shift-in-progress flag. It is
   read in four places and they agree:

   +------------+--------------+----------------+--------------+--------------------------+
   | racer+0x88 | upshift      | downshift      | torque       | multi-gear downshift     |
   +============+==============+================+==============+==========================+
   | 0          | 150 ms       | 200 ms         | x1.1         | no                       |
   +------------+--------------+----------------+--------------+--------------------------+
   | 1          | 200 ms       | 300 ms         | x1.0         | YES                      |
   +------------+--------------+----------------+--------------+--------------------------+

   o IT IS MANUAL AGAINST AUTOMATIC, and that is no longer an
     inference: the field is written from g_autoGearbox
     (0x004dc0a0), which is what the AUTO and MANUAL buttons on
     the bike-select page set.  The assisted mode shifts more
     slowly and makes less torque, and in exchange it drops
     several gears at once for you.  [verified]

   o THERE IS A FIFTH BEHAVIOUR, and it is not in the gearbox at
     all.  Racer_Create seeds racer+0xb4 from the carac at
     0x0045c569 and then adds 2.0 to THE COPY at 0x0045c594,
     gated on humanPlayer (+0x08), the solo flag (+0x04) and
     g_autoGearbox == 0.  The constant is at 0x004d0e88.  So
     manual is quicker three ways and automatic's only
     advantage is the multi-gear downshift.

9.33.2.1.  Which realSpeedMax each consumer reads

   There are TWO of them once the bonus exists - the carac field
   and the racer's bumped copy at +0xb4 - and they differ by 2.0
   for a human, solo, manual rider.  Every consumer is read
   [verified]:

   +----------------------------+------------+------------------+
   | Consumer                   | Reads      | Evidence         |
   +============================+============+==================+
   | speed clamp                | racer+0xb4 | FCOMP [ESI+0x90] |
   |                            |            | at 0x0048efb5    |
   +----------------------------+------------+------------------+
   | gearboxRoadSpeed clamp,    | racer+0xb4 | 0x0048f101 and   |
   | both signs                 |            | 0x0048f11c       |
   +----------------------------+------------+------------------+
   | Racer_SetLongSpeed, both   | racer+0xb4 | 0x0048cbe0, its  |
   | signs                      |            | opening lines    |
   +----------------------------+------------+------------------+
   | Gearbox_EngineToSpeed      | racer+0xb4 | 0x0048dd90       |
   +----------------------------+------------+------------------+
   | Gearbox_BuildShiftTable    | THE CARAC  | 0x0048e3a0 reads |
   |                            |            | g_bikeCarac[i].  |
   |                            |            | RealSpeedMax     |
   +----------------------------+------------+------------------+

   SO THE RULE IS SIMPLE: the INSTANCE's clamps use the rider's
   copy and the TABLE BUILDER uses the bike's.  Which is the
   sensible split - the shift points are a property of the bike,
   the ceiling is a property of who is riding it.

   ESI IS THE INSTANCE, racer+0x24, so instance+0x90 IS
   racer+0xb4.  That identity is what makes the four clamp sites
   readable at a glance.

   o AN A/B ON THE SHIFT TABLE PROVES NOTHING, and this was
     tried first.  MOTO in manual (ceiling 82.0) and the port
     (ceiling 80.0) produce byte-identical tables - but the cap
     term does not BIND on the stock bike, every threshold
     coming from the power crossover instead, so the test is
     insensitive to the difference.  The answer came from
     reading 0x0048e3a0, not from the comparison.

   o THE PORT USES THE CARAC EVERYWHERE, which physics.c already
     recorded as a deliberate, consistent divergence at
     physics_engine_to_speed.  It cost nothing while the two
     values were equal; in manual it caps the bike 2.0 m/s low.
     The four clamp sites are the fix; the table builder is
     already right.

   o AND IT EXPLAINS 9.20.2.  Every captured frame showed the
     1.1x not applying because every one was taken in AUTOMATIC,
     which is the bike page's default.  WHO WRITES IT was the
     open half and it is closed: FUN_00411010 at 0x004110c4
     walks the racers once as the race starts, stride 0x3209,
     and stores

         every racer but the local one    1, automatic
         the local one, solo              g_autoGearbox
         the local one, network           the player record

     so an AI is never manual.  A port that hardcodes either
     value gets a different bike.

   o THE TORQUE MULTIPLIER IS THEREFORE NOT A PROTECTION REWARD in
     the way 9.11.3 implied. It is the manual gearbox's, and the
     protection check merely gates it as well.

9.33.3.  Where the gearbox lives, and what still is not read

   maybe_Racer_UpdateAirborne CALLS ALL THREE. 9.18 read that
   function for the air timer and left its tail as "input bits 0x10
   and 0x20 drive FUN_0048e0a0 / FUN_0048e120 through the edge latch
   at 0x004ea4c8, gated on racer+0x88 - none of that is named yet".
   Those two ARE the shift requesters, so bits 0x10 and 0x20 are
   SHIFT UP and SHIFT DOWN - the game has manual shifting.

   o WHAT REQUESTS AN AUTOMATIC SHIFT IS Gearbox_RequestAutoShift
     at 0x0048e550.  It is a THIRD requester beside the two
     button-driven ones, called from the same function at
     0x00466730, and it tests road speed against the swept table:

         up    roadSpeed >  T[gear]
         down  roadSpeed <  T[gear-1] - 2

     which is what src/race.c implements.  Its own first
     statement is the manual gate: with racer+0x88 at 0 it
     builds the shift table if it has never been built and
     RETURNS, so it never requests for a manual rider.

   o THE SPEED RESYNC ON A CHANGE is the caller's job in our version.
     The original recomputes both speeds through the new ratio and,
     on an upshift, lerps the longitudinal speed by the grip factor -
     that last step needs maybe_Racer_SetLongSpeed and the body-space
     velocity. [open]

   o THE MULTI-GEAR DOWNSHIFT SEARCH is not transcribed. In mode 1
     the original walks down the ratio table while a gear's top speed
     still exceeds the road speed. [open]

9.33.4.  Transcribed

   src/physics.c gains MR_Gearbox, gearbox_request_upshift,
   gearbox_request_downshift and gearbox_tick_shift; src/race.c uses
   them instead of the instant stand-in.
   tools/check_physics.py gains six cases - that a 200 ms timer
   survives six 33 ms ticks and shifts on the seventh, that manual
   arms 150 instead, and that a top-gear request clamps rather than
   doing nothing.

9.34.  THE SPEED FAMILY IS COMPLETE - and it checks itself

   maybe_Racer_SetLongSpeed (0x0048cbe0). The third and last of the
   trio, and the one 9.33.3 named as blocking the gearbox resync.

       physics_slip_speed      READS the lateral component  (row 0)
       physics_long_speed      READS the longitudinal one   (row 2)
       physics_set_long_speed  WRITES the longitudinal one

9.34.1.  It replaces one component and keeps two

       v = clamp(v, -realSpeedMax, +realSpeedMax)
       b = (normalize(velDir) * speed - t) . rows
       world = b0*row0 + b1*row1 + v*row2 + t
       speed  = |world|
       velDir = speed > 1e-5 ? world/speed : (0,0,0)

   o b0 AND b1 SURVIVE. Only the component pairing with row 2 is
     overwritten, which is what makes this a "set the forward speed"
     primitive rather than a velocity setter - a bike can be given a
     new road speed WITHOUT LOSING ITS SLIDE. That is why the
     drivetrain can resync speed to road speed mid-corner without
     straightening the bike up.

   o SPEED IS DERIVED, NOT STORED. It is recomputed as the magnitude
     of the rebuilt world velocity every call. A reconstruction that
     keeps speed and velDir as independent state has to re-derive it
     here or they drift.

   o THE 1e-5 CONSTANT IS A DOUBLE at 0x004d1c08. Read as a float it
     is -1.4e-33, which would make the test never fire and leave a
     stopped bike with a stale direction. The plate comment had it
     right and a four-byte read did not - WHEN THE DECOMPILER WRITES
     (float)_DAT_xxxx, THE DATUM IS NOT A FLOAT.

9.34.2.  The three now check each other

   tools/check_physics.py gains a ROUND TRIP: set a longitudinal
   speed through the write side, read it back through the
   independently transcribed read side, on a yawed and tilted frame
   rather than an identity one.

       set  10.0  ->  reads 10.000000
       set  -4.0  ->  reads -4.000000
       set  37.5  ->  reads 37.500000

   o AND THE LATERAL COMPONENT IS PRESERVED ACROSS THE WRITE, checked
     with physics_slip_speed before and after: 7.200000 -> 7.200001.

   o THIS IS A DIFFERENT KIND OF EVIDENCE from the rest of the file.
     Everything in 9.17 to 9.22 was measured against the running
     game; this is two separately transcribed functions agreeing with
     each other, which catches a transposed index or a swapped row
     without needing the game at all. Both kinds are worth having and
     they fail differently.

   o IT ALSO CAUGHT A REAL GAP. The harness still had only the row-0
     reader, because when physics_slip_speed was refactored into a
     row-parameterised body (9.31.3) the mirror was never updated. A
     check that cannot express the thing it checks is not a check.

9.34.3.  What it unblocks

   The gearbox resync of 9.33.3 wanted this, as does
   maybe_Racer_UpdateGroundContact's wall-hit path and
   maybe_Racer_UpdateCrash. It is one of seven callers' shared
   primitive, so writing it moves three separate open items closer at
   once.

9.35.  THE CRASH - the rider comes off, in km/h

   maybe_Racer_UpdateCrash (0x004683e0) is the per-frame update for a
   rider who is no longer on his bike. It is a ballistics integrator
   with a bounce, plus a tumble animation, plus the thing that makes
   the bike coast to a halt while he rolls.

9.35.1.  One latch, and everything downstream of it

   The whole function is one branch [verified]:

       0x004683e8  MOV  EAX, [ECX + 0x30c1]
       0x004683f2  TEST EAX, EAX
       0x004683f4  JZ   0x00468c87            past everything

   racer+0x30c1 is crashLatch, set by maybe_Racer_BeginCrash
   (0x00467e30). It is the most consequential flag in the racer
   record, because four separate things read it: this function, the
   wheelie reset (9.23), and the drive and steering suppression in
   the tick chain (9.27).

   The LAST thing the function does before that JZ target is raise
   racer+0x319d - the flag maybe_Racer_UpdatePose reads at 0x0045e305
   to select pose block 1, mHand\corps.skl. So the hand skeleton is
   simply "the rider is off the bike", which is why the only meshes
   it adds are his hands: on the bike they are on the bars and come
   from the bike's own skeleton, and off it they need their own.

9.35.2.  The ballistics, and eight units of slack

   Per frame, with dt = g_frameDelta * 0.01 [verified]:

       pos += launchDir * riderSpeed * 128 * dt

   Then a ground test which is NOT a plain comparison. The ground
   height is run through __ftol and 8 is added:

       airborne  <=>  pos.y >= (int)groundY + 8

   The 2 constants are #defined with their addresses in
   src/physics.c.

   riderSpeed is floored at 0 on both branches, written as the
   sign-bit test 0x80000000 < (uint)x.

   THE AIRBORNE BRANCH IS WHERE DIRECTION AND SPEED ARE RECONCILED.
   Gravity is added to the DIRECTION vector, which stops it being
   unit length; the code then takes its length as the new speed and
   divides the direction by it. That is a velocity carried as a unit
   vector plus a scalar and re-split every frame - the same
   representation physics_set_long_speed rebuilds (9.34), one
   subsystem over.

   TWO OF THE THREE CONSTANTS ARE ROUND NUMBERS IN km/h [verified]:

       -4.166667 = -15/3.6      15 km/h a second, sliding
       -0.555556 =  -2/3.6       2 km/h a second, in the air
       -0.0981   = -9.81/100     gravity, carrying the dt's 1/100

   This is the third subsystem authored in km/h and converted at the
   source - Frein (9.21.1) and the slope term (9.22) were the others.
   It is now safe to treat "a suspicious decimal in the racer code"
   as "divide it by 3.6 and see if it comes out round".

9.35.3.  Two fields were swapped in the DB

   racer+0x30d5 was named crashTimer. It is not a timer. It scales
   the launch direction into a displacement, it is overwritten with
   the length of that direction after gravity, and it is what the
   drags subtract from - it is the rider's SPEED. The actual
   countdown is racer+0x30c5, which the DB left unnamed as f_30c5.

   Both are corrected in Ghidra (rule 5b): +0x30d5 is riderSpeed,
   +0x30c5 is crashTimerMs, and
   +0x30d9 - also unnamed - is riderSpeedRef, the launch speed kept
   only as the tumble-rate divisor.

   HOW THE ERROR WAS SPOTTED IS WORTH KEEPING. Nothing about the
   name looked wrong; the tell was arithmetic. A timer that gets
   multiplied by a direction vector and then reassigned from a
   vector length is not measuring time. A field's NAME is a claim
   about it and the operations it appears in are the evidence -
   when they disagree, the operations win.

9.35.4.  0.9 arrived twice, from opposite ends

   docs/formats/skl-rot.txt 5.1.9 recorded, from a trace of a downed
   rider, that his speed decayed by exactly 0.9 a frame, and used
   that to argue the hand skeleton was the crash pose. That was
   written before this function was read.

   _DAT_004d11b8 is 0.9 [verified], and it is the multiplier applied
   to the bike's speed in this function's tail. The observation and
   the binary agree on the number, on the cause, and on the
   conclusion drawn from it.

   That is worth stating plainly because most of what this project
   records has exactly one source. A trace and a static read
   reaching the same constant independently is the strongest
   evidence available under rule 8, and it is rare.

   The decay is gated, and the gate runs the other way round from how
   it first reads:

       riderSpeed >= 1/3.6  OR  crashTimerMs >= 100.0

   so the bike decays WHILE the rider is still sliding, or while the
   timer is still high - not once he has stopped. Since the timer
   starts at 400 and counts down, the first 300 units decay
   unconditionally; after that it is the rider's speed that keeps it
   going. When both have run out, FUN_004681d0 is called instead,
   which is also this function's early-out path and is unread [open].

   THE SECOND HALF IS WRITTEN AS AN INTEGER COMPARE against
   0x42c7ffff. That is the largest float below 100.0f, so the test is
   a plain "crashTimerMs >= 100.0f" done on the bit pattern to dodge
   an FPU compare. Read as a real integer it is 1.12e9 and the branch
   looks dead - which is how the gate came to be written down
   backwards here in the first place.

   On the human player the decay also resyncs the gearbox -
   SetLongSpeed, then Gearbox_SpeedToEngine - while an AI racer just
   scales its engineSpeed and one instance field directly.

9.35.5.  Transcribed, and what is not

   src/physics.c gains physics_crash_tick and
   physics_crash_speed_decay, with MR_Crash in physics.h. Both carry
   ORIGIN tags.

   Two pieces are NOT transcribed and are marked [open] in the
   source and in the plate comment:

   o THE RIDER ROOT MATRIX TUMBLE. Built from g_cosTable lookups
     composed onto matRiderRoot at a rate of
     (riderSpeed / riderSpeedRef) * dt, so the rider spins fastest
     at the moment he is thrown and stops spinning as he stops
     sliding. The decompiler renders the composition with
     extraout_ST0 and extraout_ST1 phantoms, which 9.24.4 and 9.29.1
     both established is not a transcription source. It needs a
     disassembly read.

     It costs a tumbling animation and nothing else - no simulation
     state depends on it - so it is the cheapest open item in this
     document.

   o THE WALL CLIP. The rider's move is run through
     maybe_Trk_ClipMoveAgainstWalls and riderSpeed is damped by
     _DAT_004d0f08 on a hit. The clip routine is unread.


9.36.  BEGINCRASH - one latch, and which way he falls

   maybe_Racer_BeginCrash (0x00467e30) is the transition 9.35 depends
   on: it is what sets crashLatch, and so what makes UpdateCrash do
   anything at all. It was found with a hardware watchpoint on
   racer+0x30c1, which is the only reason it has a name.

   It is ONE-SHOT. The first thing it does is return if crashLatch is
   already set, so a rider who is down cannot be re-crashed by a
   second impact in the same frame.

9.36.1.  What it sets up

   +-----------+---------------------------------+------------------+
   | Offset    | Set to                          | Source           |
   +===========+=================================+==================+
   | +0x30c1   | 1 - THE LATCH                   |                  |
   | +0x007c   | 1 - the gear, back to first     |                  |
   | +0x30c5   | 400.0 - crashTimerMs            | immediate        |
   | +0x30c9   | normalize(dir + (0, 0.6, 0))    | 0.6 at 0x4d1180  |
   | +0x30d5   | speed * 0.15 - riderSpeed       | 0.15 at 0x4d1188 |
   | +0x30d9   | the same - riderSpeedRef        |                  |
   | +0x30dd   | +pi/2 or -pi/2 - the fall side  |                  |
   | +0x30e1   | (0, fallSide, fallSide)         |                  |
   +-----------+---------------------------------+------------------+

   THE 0.6 IS AN UPWARD BIAS ADDED BEFORE THE NORMALISE [verified].
   The impact direction gets 0.6 added to its Y and is then made unit
   length, so a rider hit flat is still thrown upward and arcs
   instead of skidding - and a bigger horizontal impact tilts the
   launch flatter on its own. One multiply-add buys behaviour that
   would otherwise want a special case, which is characteristic of
   this codebase.

   THE TUMBLE RATES LEAVE X AT ZERO, so the rider rolls and yaws but
   never pitches. physics_crash_tick scales all three by
   riderSpeed/riderSpeedRef.

9.36.2.  A correction that reconciles rather than overturns

   The plate comment on this function said +0x30d5 was "THE TIMER,
   from SPEED", counted down by maybe_Racer_UpdatePose as the
   animation's progress fraction. 9.35.3 established from
   UpdateCrash's arithmetic that it is the rider's SPEED, not a
   timer.

   Both are now corrected in the DB, but the interesting part is that
   the earlier reading was RIGHT ABOUT THE BEHAVIOUR and wrong only
   about the type - and this function shows exactly why.

   +0x30d9 is set from the same expression as +0x30d5 and is then
   never written again. So the ratio

       riderSpeed / riderSpeedRef

   starts at exactly 1.0 and decays to 0 as the drags bite, and that
   ratio is what UpdateCrash scales the tumble by. There IS a
   progress fraction running the animation, and "the tumble is as
   long as you were fast" is still true. It is built out of two
   speeds rather than out of a countdown.

   The actual countdown is +0x30c5, starting at 400.

9.36.3.  The fall side is the lean

   Which way the rider goes over is a three-step fallback
   [verified]:

       racer+0x2e31 (leanTarget) if non-zero    its SIGN decides
       else racer+0x2e1d (lean)  if non-zero    its SIGN decides
       else Game_RandNext                       a coin toss

   negative giving -pi/2 and positive +pi/2. So a rider dropped
   mid-corner always goes over the way the bike was already leaning,
   and ONLY a dead-upright crash is random. That is a nice piece of
   design: the common case is deterministic and reads as physical,
   and the RNG is reserved for the one case with no answer.

   Both sign tests are written on the bit pattern
   (0x80000000 < (uint)x), which is just "is it negative"; the
   decompiler also renders the non-zero test as (int)x < 1 on the
   bits, which is "is it +0.0". Neither is an integer operation on a
   real integer.

   racer+0x2e31 is newly named leanTarget. WHAT WRITES IT WAS OPEN
   HERE AND IS NOW FOUND - see 9.41.3. maybe_Racer_UpdateGroundContact
   sets it from the WALL NORMAL at 0x0046914c, four instructions
   before it calls this function, so a rider crashed into a wall
   falls according to the wall he hit. The guess that it is a
   lean-like quantity survives; the "nothing writes it" does not.

9.36.4.  The RNG is the CRT's

   FUN_00458ce0, now Game_RandNext, returns rand(). The SIGNATURE
   given here was wrong and is corrected in 9.41.4: it is
   int __stdcall Game_RandNext(int ignored), not a no-argument
   function. The decompile that said otherwise was reading a RET 0x4
   as a plain RET.

   The behaviour is unchanged - it is still plain rand() - so the
   conclusion drawn from it in this section still holds.

   THIS MATTERS TO THE PORT OUT OF PROPORTION TO ITS SIZE. Every
   other place the game reaches for randomness can now call rand()
   and be FAITHFUL rather than substituting a stand-in - the
   sequence, the period, and the low-bit behaviour are the CRT's and
   not the game's. A reconstruction that invented its own generator
   here would be a rule 5a hack in a place nobody would think to
   look.

9.36.5.  Transcribed across two files

   Rule 13 splits it. physics.c gets physics_crash_begin - the
   timer, the biased and normalised launch direction, and the two
   speeds - because its output is a velocity. racer.c gets
   racer_begin_crash - the latch, the gear reset, the fall side, and
   the tumble rates - because it operates on one MR_Racer. Both
   carry ORIGIN tags naming the same original and the half they are.

   physics.c also gains physics_crash_decays, which is UpdateCrash's
   tail gate; see the correction in 9.35.4.

   NOT TRANSCRIBED, and noted in both files [open]:

   o the RIDER ROOT MATRIX at racer+0x3085, built from Racer_GetYaw
     composed with the +-pi/2 roll. Same gap as 9.35.5.

   o FUN_00467c40(6, position, 0xf, 0x10), an effect emitter at the
     crash point. Type 6; the emitter is unread.

   o racer+0x31a1, a second copy of the impact vector, and
     racer+0x31ad with the ghost-event flag - these look like the
     ghost recorder's business rather than the crash's.


9.37.  THE CRASH RESET - the whole field gets up at once

   FUN_004681d0, now Racer_ResetCrash, is the remount. It is what
   clears crashLatch and crashTimerMs, and maybe_Racer_UpdateCrash
   calls it from BOTH its early-out and its tail - so it is the only
   way out of the crash state, and 9.35's [open] on it is closed.

9.37.1.  Two paths, forked on humanPlayer

   +----------------------+-------------------------------------------+
   | humanPlayer != 0     | +0x180 = 0                                |
   |                      | +0x124 = 0            the m/s speed       |
   |                      | SetLongSpeed(inst, 0) the bike STOPS      |
   |                      | FUN_0048edd0(inst)                        |
   |                      | +0xec = SpeedToEngine(&+0x12c, gear,      |
   |                      |                       +0xec)              |
   +----------------------+-------------------------------------------+
   | humanPlayer == 0     | +0xec  = 0            engineSpeed         |
   |                      | +0x1a4 = 0                                |
   |                      | +0x31d1 = 200.0       0x43480000          |
   +----------------------+-------------------------------------------+

   The AI gets the cheap version plus the 200.0 recovery counter that
   a WALL HIT also sets (9.26 step 3); the human gets a real gearbox
   resync. That is the same split UpdateCrash's tail makes, so the two
   functions agree about which racer is worth the arithmetic.

   SetLongSpeed IS PASSED A LITERAL ZERO - PUSH EDI at 0x004681f9,
   with EDI already xored. Since SetLongSpeed replaces only the
   row-2 component (9.34.1), that zeroes the bike's FORWARD speed and
   keeps the other two: a bike sliding sideways when the crash ends
   keeps its drift. Reading it as "the bike stops dead" would be
   wrong.

9.37.2.  The gate, and why it is the good part

   Before doing anything, the function tests [verified]:

       this->racerIndex == g_viewedRacerIndex
       ||  racer[g_viewedRacerIndex].crashLatch == 0

   The second half is the LEA chain at 0x00468293-0x004682a9, which
   works out to 12809*g_viewedRacerIndex + 0x005842d9. Since
   0x5842d9 is 0x00581218 + 0x30c1, that address is the VIEWED
   racer's crashLatch - the multiply is by the racer stride and the
   constant carries the field offset, which is the usual shape of an
   indexed field access in this binary.

   SO A RACER ONLY REMOUNTS IF HE IS THE ONE BEING WATCHED, OR IF THE
   WATCHED RACER IS NOT HIMSELF DOWN. While the camera's racer is
   crashed, the rest of the field stays crashed with him.

9.37.3.  The cascade terminates in one level

   Only the viewed racer runs the loop (0x00468257, and again at
   0x0046834e for the AI path):

       EBP = 0x00584489                 = racer[1] + 0x68
       for EBX = 1 .. g_racerCount-1
           if EBX != this->racerIndex
              and [EBP]          != 0   racer[i].active     (+0x68)
              and [EBP + 0x3059] != 0   racer[i].crashLatch (+0x30c1)
                   LEA ECX,[EBP - 0x68] -> racer[i]
                   CALL Racer_ResetCrash
           EBP += 0x3209

   The loop pointer is kept at racer+0x68 and the guard field is
   reached as +0x3059 from there, which is +0x30c1 - the recursive
   call subtracts the 0x68 back off to get the racer base.

   THE RECURSION LOOKS UNBOUNDED AND IS NOT. A cascaded racer has
   racerIndex != g_viewedRacerIndex, so it fails the cascade test and
   only ever performs its own reset. It gets past the GATE at all
   because the viewed racer's crashLatch was cleared a few lines
   earlier - the same clear that ends his crash is what opens the
   door for everybody else.

   The gate reads a flag the body of the same function has just
   written, so the ordering is load-bearing: move the clear after the
   loop and it deadlocks.

   THE LOOP STARTS AT 1, so racer 0 is never cascaded into
   [verified from 0x00584489 being racer[1]+0x68].

   Together the gate and the cascade produce a restart that happens
   to the WHOLE FIELD AT ONCE rather than racer by racer - nobody
   remounts early and nobody is left down. The mechanism is
   verified; reading that as the intent is [inferred].

9.37.4.  A one-shot signal to the camera

   DAT_004ea510, raised on all four reset paths, has exactly six
   references in the image - four writes here and a read/write pair
   in Camera_UpdateTrackside [verified]:

       if (g_nCrashResetSignal) {
           DAT_004ea5a8   = 1;
           DAT_004dc13c   = 600;      reloads its own countdown
           cam[0x21c]     = 0;
           g_nCrashResetSignal = 0;   consumed here
       }

   DAT_004dc13c is decremented by 100 a few instructions earlier,
   guarded on a mode == 3 test, so this is what re-arms the trackside
   camera's hold when the field gets back up.

   BECAUSE THOSE SIX ARE THE ONLY REFERENCES, "one-shot signal rather
   than state flag" is verified rather than inferred - nothing else
   in the image can observe it between the raise and the clear. A
   global with two readers would not have supported that claim.

   Named g_nCrashResetSignal in the DB and g_crashResetSignal in the
   C: Ghidra's naming check requires a Hungarian prefix on globals
   and our convention does not use one. Same datum, two conventions,
   noted in race.c so nobody hunts for a second variable.

9.37.5.  Transcribed, and one divergence

   racer.c gets racer_reset_crash, the per-racer half. race.c gets
   race_reset_crashes, the gate and the cascade - it belongs there
   because it concerns more than one racer, which is rule 13's test.

   MR_Crash IS NOW EMBEDDED IN MR_Racer. It was written as a
   standalone struct in 9.35 because only one racer was being
   simulated, and the cascade is what showed that up: a function that
   walks the field needs every racer to carry its own crash state,
   and in the original they are plain racer fields at +0x30b5..+0x30e1
   anyway. The separate object made the single-racer case tidy and
   the multi-racer case impossible.

   ONE DIVERGENCE, marked [open] in the source. The original stops
   the bike through SetLongSpeed, preserving the lateral component;
   ours zeroes the speed outright, because MR_Drivetrain does not
   carry velDir. Adding one just to serve this call would be
   inventing state, so the gap is recorded instead. It costs a
   sideways drift on remount.

   Also not carried over: FUN_0048edd0(instance), called between the
   stop and the gearbox resync on the human path. Unread. [open]


9.38.  UPDATEGROUNDCONTACT - the disassembly read

   9.29 mapped maybe_Racer_UpdateGroundContact (0x00468ca0) and
   declined to transcribe it, because the decompiler loses the stack
   frame and renders every Trk_FindSegmentForPoint and
   Trk_GroundHeightAt call with an empty argument list - and which
   point is sampled where IS the algorithm.

   Read as disassembly instead, it gives up its front half without
   much argument. What follows is verified from instructions; the two
   matOrientation rebuilds are still open.

   THE FIRST THING WORTH SAYING IS THAT THE DECOMPILER WAS RIGHT TO
   BE DISTRUSTED AND ALSO NOT USELESS. 9.29.1 flagged
   "fVar17 = 4.48416e-44" as the integer 32 reinterpreted as a float.
   That was correct, and 9.38.2 is what the 32 is for. A decompile
   too broken to transcribe can still be read for tells.

9.38.1.  A second parameter the DB did not have

   The function reads [ESP+0xa8] at 0x0046942c [verified]. It is
   __fastcall with the racer in ECX, so that is a STACK ARGUMENT -
   and the DB signature had only the racer.

   Corrected in the DB (rule 5b):

       int __fastcall maybe_Racer_UpdateGroundContact(
               MR_Racer *pRacer, int bTightClearance)

   The parameter turned out to be axis0, the analog THROTTLE axis -
   see 9.44, which traced it through the callers. The provisional
   name bTightClearance described its effect here and was wrong about
   its identity; the DB now calls it nAxis0.

   A MISSING PARAMETER IS THE KIND OF ERROR THAT PROPAGATES. Every
   caller's argument list is wrong until it is fixed, and Ghidra will
   happily decompile the callers as if the extra push belonged to
   something else.

9.38.2.  The contact test is a slack band

   At 0x00469427 [verified]:

       EAX = 32
       if ([ESP+0xa8] != 0) EAX = 8
       [ESP+0x10] = EAX

       airborne  <=>  posY > (float)EAX + groundY

   So the bike counts as airborne only once it is 32 units clear of
   the ground - or 8, on the tight setting. Below that it is grounded
   and its Y is SNAPPED to the ground height at 0x004694ce.

   THAT IS THE SAME SHAPE physics_crash_tick USES, with its fixed 8
   (9.35.2). Two independent places in the racer code test "off the
   ground" as "clear by a margin" rather than "above it", and 8 is
   the value both reach for. A slack band is this codebase's house
   style for a contact test, and it is worth expecting the next time
   one turns up.

   The ground height itself comes from Trk_GroundHeightAt called with
   (0x0052c108, racer+0x31c1, &racer+0x30) - the track object, the
   START segment field, and the position - preceded by a
   Trk_FindSegmentForPoint on the same three [verified from
   0x004693f9-0x00469423]. Those are the argument lists the
   decompiler dropped.

9.38.3.  The landing frame re-seeds speed from the SPEEDOMETER

   Gated on racer+0x2dbc being clear, so it runs on the frame of
   touchdown and not on every grounded frame [verified,
   0x00469456-0x004694c4]:

       springCompression = (racer+0x40 - groundY) * -2.0
       racer+0x124       = racer+0x118 * (1/3.6)
       SetLongSpeed(instance, racer+0x124)
       FUN_0048edd0(instance)
       racer+0xec        = SpeedToEngine(&+0x12c, gear, +0xec)
       racer+0x2dac     |= 0x80

   THE SPEED IS REBUILT FROM THE NEEDLE, and that is the surprise.
   racer+0x118 is the km/h speedometer and racer+0x124 is m/s; this
   is the site that derives the second from the first. So on landing
   the PHYSICS speed is re-seeded from the DISPLAYED speed, rather
   than the display being a read-out of the physics.

   o IT CONFIRMS A NOTE THAT WAS PREVIOUSLY A GUESS. racer.h carried
     "+0x0124 the same speed in m/s, +0x118 / 3.6" with no site
     behind it. 0x00469476-0x0046948a is that site.

   o AND IT REUSES 0x004d0f70 - the same 1/3.6 that gates the crash
     decay (9.35.4). Same datum, two subsystems, which is more
     evidence for the km/h authoring of 9.35.2.

   THE -2.0 IS A DOUBLING AND A SIGN FLIP. racer+0x40 sits exactly
   where the Y of a prevPos vec3 at +0x3c would sit [inferred], and
   the expression only makes sense as an overshoot: prevPosY -
   groundY is negative on a landing, so negating gives a positive
   compression, and the 2 doubles it.

9.38.4.  The airborne fork, as far as it is read

   At 0x0046974b [verified]:

       racer+0x2db8 = 0                       onGround clear
       if (humanPlayer && racer+0x4)
           racer+0x31f9 = 0                   the stunt request
       if (racer+0x124 is +-0.0) skip to 0x004699e7
       ... normalise the move direction, cross it with (0,1,0),
           and rebuild matOrientation from that

   THE ZERO-SPEED EARLY-OUT IS WRITTEN AS TEST EAX,0x7fffffff, which
   is "is this float either +0.0 or -0.0" done on the bit pattern - a
   stationary bike in the air keeps whatever orientation it had,
   because a direction cannot be recovered from a zero velocity.
   The world-up vector is visible as the 0x3f800000 stored at
   [ESP+0x80] with zeroes either side.

9.38.5.  What is still not transcribed

   src/racer.c gains racer_is_airborne and racer_ground_land, both
   ORIGIN-tagged to this function and to the site inside it.

   STILL OPEN, and these are the expensive half:

   o BOTH matOrientation REBUILDS. Grounded, the matrix follows the
     terrain under the two wheel contacts; airborne, it follows the
     direction of travel crossed with world up. They need the
     helpers at 0x004570d0 and 0x0046d610 read first, and they are
     the input to physics_camber, physics_slip_speed and
     physics_slope - so getting them wrong would be worse than not
     having them.

   o THE WALL-HIT ARM - push out along the normal, re-find the
     segment, choose a crash or a scrape by incidence. Needs
     maybe_Trk_ClipMoveAgainstWalls, which is the same routine
     9.35.5 is waiting on.

   o FUN_0048edd0, called between the speed set and the gearbox
     resync on BOTH the landing path and racer_reset_crash. Two
     readers now, still unread.

   o THE COPY-PROTECTION CHECK stays out of src/ entirely, per 9.29.5
     and the standing instruction.


9.39.  CLIPMOVEAGAINSTWALLS - it bounces, it does not slide

   maybe_Trk_ClipMoveAgainstWalls (0x0040ca80) is the routine three
   separate open items have been waiting on - the crash's wall clip
   (9.35.5), UpdateGroundContact's wall-hit arm (9.38.5), and 9.29.3
   step 6.

   It is __thiscall on the track with six stack arguments, which RET
   0x18 confirms.

9.39.1.  The shape

       if every |from - to| component <= 1e-5      return 0

       for wall in { seg+0x60, seg+0x70 }:
           if Plane_Distance(wall, to) < 0.0:      outside
               hitPoint = intersect(wall, from, to)
               normal   = wall
               dir     -= 2 * dot(dir, normal) * normal
               normalise(dir)
               hit = 1

   The 3 constants are #defined with their addresses in
   src/trk.c.

   ALL THREE COMPONENTS MUST BE UNDER THE EPSILON for the early
   return, so a move that is tiny in x and y but large in z is still
   clipped. Written as three chained tests, each falling through to
   the work.

   BOTH WALLS ARE TESTED, not just the first match. A move into a
   corner reflects twice, and the second reflection operates on the
   already-reflected direction.

9.39.2.  The 2 is the whole finding

   The DB's plate comment on this function said, in capitals, that
   the slide was the whole point - that removing the component of the
   direction along the normal is what makes a bike scrape along a
   wall instead of stopping dead.

   THAT IS WRONG, AND THE CONSTANT IS WHAT SETTLES IT. _DAT_004d02d8
   is 2.0 [verified], so the expression is

       dir - 2 * (dir . n) * n

   which is the MIRROR IMAGE about the wall plane, not the projection
   into it. A slide would be dir - (dir . n) * n with no factor.

   The difference is the entire feel of a wall. A slide keeps most of
   the heading and lets the bike scrape along; a reflection sends it
   back out at the incident angle. Those are different games.

   READ WITHOUT THE CONSTANT, dir -= K * dot * n LOOKS EXACTLY LIKE
   A SLIDE with a stray multiply, and it is easy to assume K is 1.0
   because that is the expected formula. Here it is 2.0, and the
   difference is a bounce against a scrape.

   o BECAUSE BOTH VECTORS ARE UNIT, the reflection preserves length
     exactly, so the renormalise that follows is defensive against
     float drift rather than load-bearing. Kept anyway - it is what
     the original does, and racer+0x18c really is unit (measured
     1.000000 live).

9.39.3.  The plane argument had to be checked

   The decompiler renders both Plane_Distance calls with only the
   point, because the plane goes in ECX. Same dropped-argument tell
   as 9.38, and here it mattered: a distance test against one plane
   feeding the OTHER plane's normal would have been a silent bug in
   any transcription, and nothing in the decompile would show it.

   The disassembly at 0x0040caf0 settles it [verified]:

       ECX = (seg << 6) - seg;  ECX <<= 2       = 0xfc * seg
       EDX = this->segments
       ECX = EDX + ECX + 0x60                   -> &segment[seg].wall0
       CALL Plane_Distance (0x004b10c0)

   so the test uses the same plane whose normal is copied out - +0x60
   for the first wall and +0x70 for the second. The stride
   computation is also an independent confirmation of the 0xfc
   segment stride, arrived at as 63*4 rather than read from a struct.

9.39.4.  What it unblocks

   src/trk.c gains trk_clip_move_against_walls, with clip_one_wall as
   the body that appears twice in the original. Both ORIGIN-tagged.

   The two racer-side wall arms are now unblocked in principle -
   9.35.5's crash clip and 9.38.5's wall-hit arm - though neither is
   written yet, because both need the surrounding
   UpdateGroundContact structure rather than just this call.

   WHAT THE RACER PASSES was already recorded and is unchanged: dir
   is racer+0x18c and normal is racer+0x31c5, both unit vectors,
   neither ever written by name because both are out-parameters. The
   caller then pushes the bike out along that normal by 32.0 - the
   same 32 that is the ground clearance in 9.38.2, which is probably
   not a coincidence but is not evidence of anything either. [guess]

   A STALE COMMENT IN trk.h WENT WITH THIS PASS. It said nothing
   filled seg->ground and seg->selector and that
   trk_ground_height_at therefore returns 0.0f. That was true when
   written and stopped being true when trk_build_segment_planes was
   transcribed; trk_load has called it since. Corrected in place.


9.40.  THE matOrientation REBUILDS - one construction, two sources

   9.29.3 said the matrix is built two different ways depending on
   contact, and left both open as the expensive half of
   maybe_Racer_UpdateGroundContact. Read as disassembly, they are the
   SAME construction with a different forward vector - which is why
   the decompile looked like two unrelated blobs of FPU traffic.

   The two helpers it needed turned out to be trivial: 0x004570d0 is
   Vec3_Sub, already named, and 0x0046d610 is a plain cross product,
   now Vec3_Cross. Neither had any surprises in it. THE COST OF THE
   OPEN ITEM WAS NOT THE HELPERS - it was that nobody had tracked the
   stack slots through the pushes to see which vector went where.

9.40.1.  The construction

       forward = normalize(source)
       lateral = normalize(worldUp x forward)
       up      = normalize(forward x lateral)
       rows    = { lateral, up, forward }

   +------------+------------------------------------------------+
   | Arm        | source                                         |
   +============+================================================+
   | grounded   | frontContact - rearContact, both snapped to    |
   | 0x00469582 | the ground under their own segments            |
   +------------+------------------------------------------------+
   | airborne   | racer+0x18c, the movement direction            |
   | 0x0046974b |                                                |
   +------------+------------------------------------------------+

   worldUp is built on the stack as a literal (0, 1, 0) - the
   0x3f800000 store at 0x004696a0 with zeroes either side, and again
   at 0x004697c7 on the airborne arm.

   THE AIRBORNE ARM COPIES ITS SOURCE WITH A SUBTRACTION. It calls
   Vec3_Sub against a temporary that was just zeroed, so the result
   is racer+0x18c unchanged. Not an optimisation anyone would write
   deliberately; it reads like the same code shape as the grounded
   arm with the second operand degenerated to zero.

9.40.2.  The bike cannot roll from the terrain

   lateral is derived from worldUp on BOTH arms, every frame. Since
   the whole basis is then built out of that, the frame this function
   produces has pitch and yaw and NO ROLL - a bike crossing a
   banked surface stays upright in this matrix.

   THAT SETTLES WHAT MR_Racer.txt MEANT by "pitch and yaw come from
   the two wheel contact points, roll cannot", and it says the reason
   is not the contact points at all - two points do define a roll-free
   frame, but the actual cause is that the third axis is anchored to
   world up rather than to anything the track supplies. The airborne
   arm has no contact points and is equally roll-free.

   So the lean is composed in somewhere else, by a different matrix.
   That is consistent with 9.29.3's note that the bike root matrix at
   +0x2e55 is separate.

9.40.3.  The row order is now read, not inferred

   The stores at 0x004696f2-0x00469746 [verified]:

       racer+0xbc, +0xc0, +0xc4   <-  lateral      row 0
       racer+0xc8, +0xcc, +0xd0   <-  up           row 1
       racer+0xd4, +0xd8, +0xdc   <-  forward      row 2

   physics.h has been asserting "row1 is matOrientation's UP axis and
   row2 its FORWARD" on the strength of how those rows are USED -
   body_axis_speed takes row 2 for the longitudinal component, row 0
   for the lateral. That was sound reasoning and it was still
   inference. This is the write side, and it agrees.

   A CONVENTION CONFIRMED FROM BOTH ENDS IS WORTH THE FEW MINUTES.
   Every one of physics_camber, physics_slip_speed, physics_long_speed
   and physics_slope indexes this matrix by row, so a transposed
   reading would have been wrong everywhere at once and consistently
   - the hardest kind of error to notice.

9.40.4.  Each wheel is sampled in its OWN segment

   Before taking the ground height, the original re-runs
   Trk_FindSegmentForPoint for the front and rear contact points
   SEPARATELY (0x00469609 and 0x00469630), then calls
   Trk_GroundHeightAt on each with its own segment
   (0x00469642, 0x00469656). Each call's result is stored straight
   back into that point's Y.

   SO A BIKE STRADDLING A SEGMENT BOUNDARY GETS ITS FRONT WHEEL ON
   ONE PIECE OF ROAD AND ITS REAR ON THE OTHER, which is what lets
   the frame pitch smoothly across a crest instead of snapping when
   the racer's own segment index changes. Sampling both in the
   racer's segment would put a visible hitch at every boundary.

   The re-find is skipped when the tight-clearance parameter is set
   and an internal flag is clear (0x00469601). That is a THIRD thing
   that parameter does - 9.38.1 knew only about the clearance - and
   it is still unexplained. [open]

9.40.5.  Transcribed, with two divergences

   src/racer.c gains racer_frame_from_forward - the shared
   construction - plus racer_orient_airborne and
   racer_orient_grounded for the two sources, and vec3_cross /
   vec3_normalize as local helpers. All ORIGIN-tagged to the site
   inside UpdateGroundContact rather than just to the function.

   TWO DELIBERATE DIVERGENCES, both marked [open] in the source:

   o A DEGENERATE forward returns 0 and leaves the matrix alone. The
     original does not guard it: a zero-length or up-parallel forward
     gives a zero lateral, Vec3_Normalize's own divide check leaves
     that zero, and the result is a SINGULAR matOrientation. Ours
     refuses instead, because that matrix feeds physics_camber,
     physics_slip_speed and physics_slope simultaneously and the
     failure would surface a long way from here.

     This is a case where matching the original exactly would import
     a latent bug. Flagged rather than hidden, so a later reader
     can decide against it with the reason in front of it.

   o Both contacts were sampled in the RACER's segment, not their
     own, because Trk_FindSegmentForPoint was not transcribed. THAT
     IS NOW CLOSED - see 9.43. racer_orient_grounded calls it once
     per contact, so the two wheels can sit on different pieces of
     road exactly as the original intends.


9.41.  THE WALL-HIT ARM - one in four, and only in the air

   The last structural piece of maybe_Racer_UpdateGroundContact.
   trk_clip_move_against_walls (9.39) is the test; this is what the
   racer does with the answer.

9.41.1.  The clip runs twice

   Two call sites, 0x00468fe9 and 0x0046909f [verified]:

       hit = clip(seg = EDI, from = racer+0x3c, to = racer+0x30, ...)

       if (hit and FindSegmentForPoint(hitPoint) == EDI)
           go straight to the payload
       else
           restore dir to its pre-clip value
           hit = clip(seg = racer+0x31c1, ...)

       if (!hit) carry on to the ground test

   SO A HIT WHOSE HIT POINT LANDS IN A DIFFERENT SEGMENT IS THROWN
   AWAY AND REDONE against the racer's own segment field, from the
   ORIGINAL direction - the saved copy at [ESP+0x10..0x18] is written
   back before the retry. A miss is also retried. Only a hit that
   stays inside the segment it was tested against is trusted first
   time.

   THE `from` IS racer+0x3c AND THE `to` IS racer+0x30. That settles
   what +0x3c is: the PREVIOUS position. 9.38.3 had it as [inferred]
   from the landing impact using +0x40 as a prevPos.y; passing
   &racer+0x3c as a move's start against the live position as its end
   makes it [verified], and +0x40 is its Y as expected.

9.41.2.  The payload

   At 0x004690ae [verified]:

       position       = hitPoint + normal * 32.0
       racer+0x31c1   = FindSegmentForPoint(position)
       racer+0x174..  = the reflected direction
       leanTarget     = FUN_0046ad10(racer, normal, -angle)

       if (onGroundPrev == 0):                 hit while AIRBORNE
           if ((rand() & 3) == 0)  CRASH       BeginCrash; bits |= 2
           else                    SCRAPE      bits |= 1
           racer+0x180 = speed * 0.8, floored at 2/3.6
           speed       = 0

   The 3 constants are #defined with their addresses in
   src/racer.c.

   THE CRASH IS A ONE-IN-FOUR COIN, written as TEST AL,0x3 with a JNZ
   to the scrape - so only a rand() whose low two bits are both clear
   crashes you.

   AND THE WHOLE FORK IS BEHIND onGroundPrev BEING CLEAR. A bike
   already on the ground that touches a wall never reaches it; it
   takes a separate path at 0x004691cf which is not read yet [open].
   So the walls punish you for hitting them while jumping, not for
   leaning on them - which is a real gameplay rule and not one that
   would be guessed from the outside.

   THE SPEED GOES SOMEWHERE. racer+0x180 takes 80 per cent of it,
   floored at two km/h, and the bike's own speed is then ZEROED.
   Since physics_crash_tick decays +0x180 by 0.9 and
   racer_reset_crash clears it, it reads as where the momentum is
   parked across an impact. [inferred]

   The 32.0 pushback is the same 32 that is the ground clearance in
   9.38.2. One "bike-sized unit" used twice, or a coincidence -
   nothing decides it. [guess]

9.41.3.  leanTarget's writer, found

   9.36.3 named racer+0x2e31 leanTarget from the fact that
   maybe_Racer_BeginCrash tests its sign AHEAD of lean to pick which
   way a rider falls, and recorded that nothing was known to write
   it.

   This function writes it, at 0x0046914c [verified]:

       FUN_00490220(&racer+0xbc)      an angle off matOrientation
       FCHS                           negated
       racer+0x2e31 = FUN_0046ad10(racer, normal, -angle)

   and it does so FOUR INSTRUCTIONS BEFORE calling BeginCrash at
   0x00469174. So the value BeginCrash reads to choose the fall side
   is the one this line just computed from THE WALL NORMAL.

   A RIDER CRASHED INTO A WALL FALLS ACCORDING TO THE WALL HE HIT,
   not according to how he was leaning. 9.36.3 read the fallback
   chain as "leanTarget, else lean, else a coin" and guessed
   leanTarget was a commanded lean; the chain is right and the guess
   was too narrow. In the wall case leanTarget is a collision
   response, and it takes priority precisely because a wall is a
   better predictor of which way you go over than your own lean was
   a moment earlier.

   Both helpers are still unread, so the value is not transcribed -
   FUN_00490220 (an angle from the orientation matrix) and
   FUN_0046ad10 (racer, normal, angle -> a lean). [open]

9.41.4.  Two corrections

   o Game_RandNext IS NOT A NO-ARGUMENT FUNCTION. 9.36.4 gave it as
     void FUN_00458ce0(void) on the decompiler's word. The
     disassembly is CALL 0x004b3600 / RET 0x4, so it is
     int __stdcall Game_RandNext(int ignored).

     THE ARGUMENT IS PROVABLY DISCARDED. The thunk pushes nothing
     before its CALL, so rand() sees this function's return address
     on top of the stack - if it read a first argument it would get
     the caller's return address. Callers pass one anyway; this site
     pushes the immediate 0x18b1, which looks like a leftover range
     or call-site tag. [inferred]

     The behaviour claim - plain rand(), so the port can call rand()
     and be faithful - is unaffected. Only the signature was wrong,
     and the DB is corrected.

   o BOTH CALLERS USE ONLY THE LOW BITS - bit 0 for the fall side,
     bits 0..1 for the crash roll. That is the weakest part of a
     linear congruential rand(), and worth remembering if either ever
     looks non-random in play.

9.41.5.  Transcribed

   src/racer.c gains racer_wall_hit. MR_Racer gains prevPos[3] at
   +0x3c - replacing the single prevPosY, now that the offset is
   verified - and moveDirClipped[3] at +0x174, which nothing has been
   found to read yet [open].

   NOT TRANSCRIBED: the leanTarget write (9.41.3), the grounded-wall
   path at 0x004691cf, and the segment re-find, which still wants
   Trk_FindSegmentForPoint.

   WHAT THIS FINISHES. With 9.38, 9.40 and this section,
   maybe_Racer_UpdateGroundContact is read end to end apart from
   those three - the contact test, the landing, both matrix rebuilds
   and the wall arm are all in src/. It was the largest open item in
   this document when 9.29 was written.


9.42.  THE GROUNDED-WALL PATH - a severity ladder

   9.41.2 left the branch at 0x004691cf unread - what happens when a
   bike that was ALREADY ON THE GROUND touches a wall. It is not the
   airborne rule with different numbers. It is a different rule.

9.42.1.  Three ways to crash, hardest first

   The path opens by measuring how square the hit was [verified,
   0x004691cf-0x004691f3]:

       incidence = |dot(wallNormal, dir)|

   with dir the reflected direction out of the clip. The result is
   ABS'd by masking the sign bit - AND [ESP+0x2c],0x7fffffff - so
   0 is a glancing blow along the wall and 1 is dead head-on.

   Then, in order:

   +--------+--------------------------------+---------------------+
   | Tier   | Condition                      | Result              |
   +========+================================+=====================+
   | 1      | incidence > 0.98               | CRASH, no dice      |
   |        | AND speed > 100 km/h           | rolled at all       |
   +--------+--------------------------------+---------------------+
   | 2      | (rand() & 3) == 0              | CRASH               |
   |        | AND incidence > 0.5            |                     |
   |        | AND speed > 80 km/h            |                     |
   +--------+--------------------------------+---------------------+
   | 3      | unk307d != 0                   | CRASH               |
   |        | AND speed > 20 km/h            |                     |
   +--------+--------------------------------+---------------------+
   | else   |                                | SCRAPE              |
   +--------+--------------------------------+---------------------+

   and either way, crash or scrape, speed *= 0.75.

   The 5 constants are #defined with their addresses in
   src/racer.c.

   TIER 1 IS THE ONLY UNCONDITIONAL CRASH IN THE FUNCTION. Hit a
   wall square on at over 100 km/h and no random number is consulted.

   racer+0x307d, tier 3's gate, is a float tested only for being
   non-zero (TEST EAX,0x7fffffff, the +-0.0 test again). Nothing
   found so far writes it, so what makes a slow hit crashy is
   unknown. [open]

9.42.2.  Every threshold is round in km/h

   100, 80 and 20, all divided by 3.6 and stored as doubles. That is
   now the FIFTH place in this subsystem authored in km/h and
   converted at the source - after Frein (9.21.1), the slope term
   (9.22), the crash drags (9.35.2) and the wall rebound floor
   (9.41.2).

   The habit is consistent enough to use as a tool rather than a
   curiosity: on meeting an unexplained decimal anywhere in the racer
   code, multiply by 3.6 before anything else. It has now paid out
   five times and cost one multiplication each time.

9.42.3.  The two arms are different rules

   +-------------------+------------------------------------------+
   | Airborne          | a flat 1-in-4 coin. No angle test, no    |
   | (9.41.2)          | speed test. Speed is ZEROED and 80 per   |
   |                   | cent of it parked in unk0180.            |
   +-------------------+------------------------------------------+
   | Grounded          | a three-tier ladder on incidence and     |
   | (here)            | speed. Speed is scaled by 0.75 and       |
   |                   | unk0180 is not touched.                  |
   +-------------------+------------------------------------------+

   IN THE AIR A WALL IS A LOTTERY; ON THE GROUND IT IS A TEST YOU CAN
   PASS - by hitting the wall at a shallow angle, or slowly, or both.
   That is the right way round for a racing game: almost all wall
   contact happens while riding, so the grounded case is the one that
   has to feel fair, and it is the one that got the detailed rule.

   It also explains a thing 9.41.2 stated but could not justify -
   that walls "punish you for hitting them while jumping". They do,
   and now the reason is visible: the airborne arm skips every
   mitigating test the grounded arm applies.

9.42.4.  Transcribed, and what is left

   racer_wall_hit in src/racer.c now covers both arms; MR_Racer gains
   unk307d. The airborne arm was already there from 9.41.

   TWO THINGS REMAIN in this branch, both [open]:

   o What writes racer+0x307d.

   o The tail past 0x004692e4, which takes another dot product of
     the direction against a stack vector at [ESP+0x98] and compares
     it to 0x004d0e54. It runs after the speed scaling on both the
     crash and scrape paths, and is not read.

   maybe_Racer_UpdateGroundContact now has exactly four open items
   across 9.38, 9.40, 9.41 and this section: that tail, the
   leanTarget write, Trk_FindSegmentForPoint, and what the
   bTightClearance parameter is FOR. Everything else in a
   4026-byte function is in src/.


9.43.  TRK_FINDSEGMENTFORPOINT - a walk, not a search

   Trk_FindSegmentForPoint (0x0040c7e0) is called five times in
   maybe_Racer_UpdateGroundContact alone and once more in
   maybe_Racer_UpdateCrash, which is why it was worth doing next.
   __thiscall on the track with three stack arguments; RET 0xc.

9.43.1.  How it works

       if (segmentCount <= 0) return 0
       if (seg >= segmentCount) seg = 0

       d = plane_distance(segments[seg].planes[0], p)

       d >= 0   walk FORWARD while the NEXT segment's d is also >= 0;
                return the LAST one that was
       d <  0   walk BACKWARD until d >= 0; return that one

   IT IS A LINEAR WALK FROM A HINT AND THAT IS THE DESIGN, not a
   shortcoming. Every caller already knows roughly where the point is
   - last frame's segment, or the racer's own +0x31c1 - so the walk is
   almost always one or two steps. A track with hundreds of segments
   never needs a search because nothing ever moves more than a
   segment or two per frame.

   planes[0] is the segment's ENTRY plane; trk_load overwrites the
   file's plane0 with one built from the segment's own position and
   direction. So d >= 0 reads as "the point has passed this segment's
   start", and the answer is the last segment it has entered but
   whose successor it has not. The forward and backward arms are the
   same statement approached from either side.

   BOTH WALKS WRAP - (seg+1) % count forward, a manual wrap to
   count-1 backward - which is what makes it work on a closed
   circuit.

   THE PLANE ARGUMENT IS IN ECX AND THE DECOMPILER DROPS IT, so all
   three Plane_Distance calls render bare. Third function in a row
   with that tell (9.38, 9.39, this). The disassembly at 0x0040c80c
   shows the usual stride:

       EAX = seg; EAX <<= 6; EAX -= seg      = 63*seg
       ECX = segments + EAX*4 + 0x40         = &segments[seg].plane0

9.43.2.  Two things with no guard

   o NEITHER WALK IS BOUNDED. If every entry plane reported the same
     sign - degenerate planes, or a point nowhere near the track -
     the loop never terminates.

   o seg IS CLAMPED ONLY ON THE HIGH SIDE. A negative hint indexes
     before the array.

   Neither is reachable with well-formed data and a sane hint, and
   the original is not wrong to omit them. Our version bounds both
   walks at segmentCount and clamps the index both ways, which is a
   DELIBERATE DIVERGENCE recorded in trk.h.

   THE REASON IS THAT A HANG IS THE WORST FAILURE MODE AVAILABLE. A
   wrong segment produces a visibly wrong ground height that points
   straight at this function; an infinite loop produces a frozen
   process and no information whatsoever. Paying one comparison per
   step to convert the second into the first is worth it, and it is
   the same reasoning as the singular-matrix guard in 9.40.5.

9.43.3.  What it closes

   src/trk.c gains trk_find_segment_for_point.

   AND THE 9.40.5 DIVERGENCE IS RESOLVED. racer_orient_grounded was
   sampling both wheel contacts in the racer's own segment, because
   this function did not exist; it now calls it once per contact, so
   the front and rear wheels sit on their own pieces of road. That
   restores the behaviour 9.40.4 identified - the frame pitching
   smoothly across a crest instead of hitching when the racer's
   segment index changes.

   THAT IS THE PAYOFF FOR DOING THE SHARED ROUTINE RATHER THAN THE
   NEXT PIECE OF THE CALLER. It was one of four open items in
   UpdateGroundContact and it also silently fixed a defect in code
   already written, in a different function, that nothing would have
   flagged.

   maybe_Racer_UpdateGroundContact is now down to three open items:
   the tail past 0x004692e4, the leanTarget write, and what the
   bTightClearance parameter is for.


9.44.  THE SECOND PARAMETER IS axis0, AND IT IS NOT A FLAG

   9.38.1 found that maybe_Racer_UpdateGroundContact reads a stack
   argument the DB signature did not have, and called it
   bTightClearance after the only thing it was seen to do. 9.40.4 and
   9.42 then found it doing two more things. Reading the callers
   settles all three at once, and the answer is not a flag.

9.44.1.  Three levels of call chain

   The function has exactly ONE caller, which is what makes this
   tractable [verified]:

   +----------------------+--------------------------------------------+
   | Race_TickAllRacers   | reads racer[i]+0x31f1 into ECX and pushes  |
   | 0x0047dfc9           | it as the second of four arguments         |
   +----------------------+--------------------------------------------+
   | Racer_TickDispatch   | forwards it unchanged as arg2 of           |
   | 0x0045d56d           | maybe_Racer_Tick                           |
   +----------------------+--------------------------------------------+
   | maybe_Racer_Tick     | loads arg2 into EBX at 0x0045d705 and      |
   | 0x0045d799           | pushes EBX straight into this function     |
   +----------------------+--------------------------------------------+

   THE OFFSETS IN Race_TickAllRacers ARE WORTH SHOWING, because they
   are the same indexed-field shape as everywhere else in this
   binary - a stride multiply with the field offset folded into the
   displacement:

       [ECX + EDX*8 + 0x584401]   racer+0x31e9   inputWord
       [ECX + EDX*8 + 0x584405]   racer+0x31ed   inputWord2
       [ECX + EDX*8 + 0x584409]   racer+0x31f1   axis0
       [ECX + EDX*8 + 0x58440d]   racer+0x31f5   axis1

   with 0x581218 the racer base, so 0x584409 - 0x581218 = 0x31f1. The
   two input words are OR'd together into the buttons argument and
   the two axes are passed separately.

   And 9.9.1 settled racer+0x31f1 already: axis0 is the analog
   THROTTLE, axis1 the steering, both centred at 0x8000.

9.44.2.  What it does with it

   All three uses test it with TEST EAX,EAX or TEST EDI,EDI - as a
   boolean:

   +--------------+----------------------------------------------+
   | 0x00469427   | clearance = axis0 ? 8 : 32                   |
   +--------------+----------------------------------------------+
   | 0x00469601   | skip the per-wheel segment re-find when      |
   |              | axis0 is non-zero and an internal flag clear |
   +--------------+----------------------------------------------+

   SO A GROUND-CONTACT TOLERANCE IS BEING SELECTED BY THE THROTTLE
   POSITION. That is not a sentence anyone would write on purpose. It
   reads as a parameter list carried down a call chain - buttons,
   axis0, axis1, dt, forwarded verbatim through two functions - and
   then the second slot reused at the bottom for something it was
   never meant to carry.

   A NAME TAKEN FROM AN EFFECT ASSERTS THAT THE EFFECT IS THE INTENT.
   Here it is not, so the DB calls it nAxis0 - which asserts only
   what is verified.

9.44.3.  RESOLVED - the 32 is dead

   The question left here was what an axis0 of exactly ZERO means at
   runtime, since that decides whether the 32-unit clearance is
   reachable. 9.45 answers it: axis0 is 0x8000 on every path a
   keyboard machine takes, so the test is non-zero and 8 is always
   chosen. The 32 is reachable only by a joystick throttle sitting at
   exactly its zero extreme.

   HAD IT GONE THE OTHER WAY it would have been a real gameplay
   difference between input devices, invisible to anyone reading
   either function alone.


9.45.  THE INPUT LAYER - where the axes come from

   maybe_Game_ReadInput, the body at 0x004116f0 behind the thunk at
   0x0047ec40. game-object.txt has it as vtable slot +0x10, the input
   stage of the nine-slot interface. 9.8 recorded that the tick reads
   inputs out of the RACER rather than out of a controller object;
   this is the function that puts them there.

9.45.1.  The per-step reset

   Before anything else it clears the local player's record, at
   0x00584401 + g_inputRacerIndex * 0x3209 [verified]:

       racer+0x31ed  = 0          inputWord2
       racer+0x31f1  = 0x8000     axis0
       racer+0x31f5  = 0x8000     axis1

   racer+0x31e9, the button word Input_OnKeyDown ORs into, is NOT
   reset here - which is consistent with 9.x's note that the key
   handler accumulates into it and something else clears it.

9.45.2.  The device fork

   In live play the loop at 0x00411877 walks every racer from 1. The
   walk pointer starts at 0x0058760e, which is racer[1]+0x31ed, so
   the negative indices name two fields that were previously unmapped
   [verified]:

   +---------------+---------------+-----------------------------+
   | Expression    | Racer offset  | Field                       |
   +===============+===============+=============================+
   | puVar4[-3]    | +0x31e1       | the DEVICE TYPE             |
   +---------------+---------------+-----------------------------+
   | puVar4[-2]    | +0x31e5       | a device index, x0x38 into  |
   |               |               | the table at 0x0051bcf4     |
   +---------------+---------------+-----------------------------+
   | puVar4[0]     | +0x31ed       | inputWord2                  |
   +---------------+---------------+-----------------------------+
   | puVar4[1]     | +0x31f1       | axis0, the throttle         |
   +---------------+---------------+-----------------------------+
   | puVar4[2]     | +0x31f5       | axis1, the steering         |
   +---------------+---------------+-----------------------------+

   and the fork is:

       if (deviceType == 2 || deviceType == 4)      the joystick types
           inputWord2 = maybe_Input_ReadDevice(i)
           axis0 = [0x0051bcf8 + deviceIndex*0x38]
           axis1 = [0x0051bcf4 + deviceIndex*0x38]
           if (DAT_005429b0 != 1) { axis1 = 0x8000; inputWord2 &= 1 }
       else
           inputWord2 = 0
           axis0 = 0x8000
           axis1 = 0x8000

   SO A NON-JOYSTICK RACER GETS BOTH AXES CENTRED, every step, from
   two different places - the reset above and this else-arm.

9.45.3.  Confirmed live, on a keyboard-only machine

   Read straight out of the running process at racer[1]+0x31e1,
   twenty-four bytes [verified]:

       +0x31e1   00000001    deviceType   - not 2, not 4
       +0x31e5   00000000    deviceIndex
       +0x31e9   00000000    inputWord
       +0x31ed   00000000    inputWord2
       +0x31f1   00008000    axis0
       +0x31f5   00008000    axis1

   deviceType 1 is what a machine with no joystick reports, and both
   axes sit at centre exactly as the else-arm writes them. Static
   read and live read agree.

   TAKEN TWICE - once on the starting grid during the countdown, and
   once after the race had started and the player had accelerated.
   Byte-for-byte identical both times. So this is not the initialised
   state being read back; it is what the field holds while the game
   is running and being driven.

   inputWord READ 0 IN BOTH SAMPLES, and that is an artefact of the
   method rather than a finding: the player has to leave the game
   window to report back, which releases every key. It does not
   affect the axis result, because maybe_Game_ReadInput writes the
   axes unconditionally every step whether or not any key is down.
   Worth stating so nobody later reads "inputWord = 0 while
   accelerating" out of this table as a fact about the game.

9.45.4.  What it settles and what it opens

   o 9.44.3 IS RESOLVED. maybe_Racer_UpdateGroundContact's 32-unit
     ground clearance is effectively dead code: it is chosen only
     when axis0 is zero, and axis0 is 0x8000 for every keyboard
     player. Only a joystick throttle at exactly its zero extreme
     reaches it. The transcription keeps both branches anyway - the
     other one IS reachable, just not here.

   o TWO FIELDS ARE NEWLY NAMED - racer+0x31e1 deviceType and
     +0x31e5 deviceIndex - and both are in MR_Racer in src/ now.

   o race.c's r->axis0 = 0x8000 IS VINDICATED. It was written as a
     placeholder with a comment saying there is no analog stick; it
     turns out to be exactly what the original writes for a keyboard
     player, so the reconstruction was accidentally right and is now
     deliberately right.

   o STILL OPEN: DAT_005429b0. When it is not 1 the joystick arm
     forces axis1 to centre and masks the buttons to bit 0, reducing
     a stick to throttle-only. What mode that is has not been read.

   o STILL OPEN: the device table at 0x0051bcf4, stride 0x38, and
     maybe_Input_ReadDevice. Neither is needed for a keyboard port,
     which is why they are noted rather than chased.


9.46.  THE WALL TAIL - one measurement used twice

   The last unread piece of maybe_Racer_UpdateGroundContact, from
   0x004692e4. It runs only on the GROUNDED arm; the airborne one
   jumps past it (0x004691ca) straight to the final SetLongSpeed.

9.46.1.  Glance or rebound

   It forks on the SIGN of a dot product between the reflected
   direction and a vec3 the original keeps at [ESP+0x98], against a
   threshold of 0.0 (0x004d0e54) [verified]:

       dot >= 0    A GLANCE - still travelling roughly the old way
           moveDirClipped = clippedDir
           speed *= (1.0 - incidence) * 0.9

       dot <  0    A REBOUND - the bounce turned you around
           unk0180 = speed * 0.5, floored at 5 km/h
           speed  *= 0.3

       then speed is floored at 1 km/h either way, and
       SetLongSpeed(instance, speed) writes it back.

   +------------+----------+--------------------------------------+
   | Address    | Value    | Role                                 |
   +============+==========+======================================+
   | 0x004d0e54 | 0.0      | the dot threshold - just the sign    |
   +------------+----------+--------------------------------------+
   | 0x004d0e70 | 1.0      | what incidence is subtracted from    |
   +------------+----------+--------------------------------------+
   | 0x004d11b8 | 0.9      | the glance multiplier - SAME datum   |
   |            |          | as the crash speed decay, 9.35.4     |
   +------------+----------+--------------------------------------+
   | 0x004d0f08 | 0.5      | what goes into unk0180 on a rebound  |
   +------------+----------+--------------------------------------+
   | 0x004d11e8 | 5/3.6    | the unk0180 floor threshold          |
   +------------+----------+--------------------------------------+
   | 0x3fb1c71c | 5/3.6    | the unk0180 floor VALUE - the same   |
   +------------+----------+--------------------------------------+
   | 0x004d11f8 | 0.3      | the rebound speed multiplier         |
   +------------+----------+--------------------------------------+
   | 0x004d0f70 | 1/3.6    | the speed floor threshold            |
   +------------+----------+--------------------------------------+
   | 0x3e8e38e4 | 1/3.6    | the speed floor VALUE - the same     |
   +------------+----------+--------------------------------------+

   THE GLANCE FORMULA IS THE GOOD PART: speed *= (1 - incidence). A
   blow along the wall has incidence near 0 and costs almost nothing;
   a square one has incidence near 1 and takes almost everything.

   AND IT IS THE SAME incidence THE CRASH LADDER USES. 9.42.1 found
   it deciding WHETHER you crash; this decides HOW MUCH SPEED YOU
   LOSE. One measurement, two consequences, and they agree with each
   other by construction - you cannot have a hit that crashes you
   without also costing you the speed, or one that costs nothing but
   puts you down.

   BOTH FLOORS FOLLOW THE HOUSE PATTERN of threshold and clamp value
   being the SAME number, seen already at 9.41.2. And both are round
   in km/h - 5 and 1 - which is the sixth place in this subsystem.

   refDir IS NOT IDENTIFIED. Its only job is to pick an arm, and it
   behaves like the pre-collision heading. The transcription takes it
   as a parameter and treats NULL as the glance arm. [open]

9.46.2.  What it completes

   racer_wall_hit gains the tail and a refDir parameter.

   maybe_Racer_UpdateGroundContact IS NOW READ END TO END. A
   4026-byte function that 9.29 declined to transcribe at all - on
   the correct grounds that the decompiler loses its stack frame -
   is in src/ across 9.38, 9.40, 9.41, 9.42, 9.43 and this section.

   THE ONE THING DELIBERATELY LEFT OUT is the g_windowTokenOk copy
   protection check (9.29.5), per the standing instruction.

   ONE OPEN ITEM REMAINS: the leanTarget write at 0x0046914c, which
   needs FUN_00490220 and FUN_0046ad10. Its absence costs the wall
   crash its fall-side (9.41.3); everything else in the function
   works without it.

   THE METHOD LESSON, since this took six sections: the decompiler
   was right to be distrusted and the disassembly was legible
   throughout. Nothing in this function was hard once read as
   instructions - the difficulty was entirely that the decompiled
   form hid the argument lists and the stack slots, and the fix was
   to stop reading the decompile rather than to work harder at it.


9.47.  TICKAI - the AI is placed, not simulated

   maybe_Racer_TickAI (0x0046bf80), 2076 bytes, the other arm of
   Racer_TickDispatch's fork on humanPlayer. 9.26.4 mapped its shape;
   this reads it.

   ITS DECOMPILE IS GOOD, unlike UpdateGroundContact's - the struct is
   applied and the calls carry their arguments. Worth noting because
   the last six sections established a habit of distrusting the
   decompiler in this subsystem, and the habit should not harden into
   a rule. Check the tells, then decide.

9.47.1.  What it does not call

   No drive, no steering, no cornering, no drivetrain. FUN_0046b120
   moves the rider along the racing line, speed is assigned from
   engineSpeed outright, and the bike is snapped to the ground with
   matOrientation rebuilt from scratch every frame rather than
   accumulated.

   THE CONSEQUENCE THAT MATTERS MOST: nothing but the player goes
   through the drivetrain. A bug in physics_tick_drivetrain is
   invisible on all 24 other bikes, and feeding the AI the player's
   physics would not reproduce it, and it is why every live capture
   in 9.17-9.22 watched racer 1 - there was nothing to see anywhere
   else.

9.47.2.  The wall response is far simpler

       position = hitPoint
       segment  = find(position)
       engineSpeed *= 0.5            0x004d1018
       unk31d1  = 200.0              0x004d101c
       riderStateBits |= 1

   That is the whole thing. Where the player gets the three-tier
   severity ladder of 9.42 and the glance/rebound tail of 9.46, an AI
   loses half its engine speed and takes the scrape bit.

   AI RIDERS CANNOT BE KNOCKED OFF THEIR BIKES BY A WALL. There is no
   Game_RandNext roll and no call to BeginCrash on this path at all.

   THE GROUND CLEARANCE USES THE SAME 8 AND 32 as the player
   (0x004d1020 and 0x004d1024), but chooses between them on a
   stunt/track condition rather than on axis0. That is a useful
   cross-check on 9.44: the pair really is a genuine pair of
   ride-height tolerances, used deliberately in two places, and not
   an artefact of the odd axis0 test.

9.47.3.  The lean AMPLIFIES, it does not decay

   The DB's plate comment described this block as "lean: decay toward
   zero clamped to +-PI/4". THE MULTIPLIERS ARE ABOVE 1 [verified]:

       0x004d1038 = 1.5    persoAnim riders
       0x004d0fe0 = 1.2    everyone else

   so it drives the lean OUTWARD to the clamp, not inward to zero.
   Corrected in the DB.

       if (!persoAnim and unk31d1 != 0)   lean = 0, SKIPPING the clamp
       else                               lean *= 1.2 or 1.5, clamped
                                          to +-pi/4
       lean = leanPrev + (lean - leanPrev) * 0.5
       leanPrev = lean

   maybe_Racer_UpdateLean runs immediately before and sets the value
   this amplifies, so the pair reads as "pick a direction, then commit
   to it hard" - which is why AI bikes lean so much more decisively
   than the player's does.

   A WALL HIT SNAPS AN ORDINARY AI UPRIGHT, because the wall sets
   unk31d1 to 200.0 and a non-zero unk31d1 takes the lean to zero.
   It stays upright while that counter runs down. persoAnim riders
   are exempt.

   THE LERP RUNS ON EVERY BRANCH, upright included - the zero arm
   jumps to 0x0046c723 which falls straight into it. Reading the
   clamp-skipping jump as an early exit would have left leanPrev
   stale.

   BOTH pi/4 CLAMPS FOLLOW THE HOUSE PATTERN, threshold and value the
   same number (0x004d1218 and 0x004d12c8 against the 0.7853982
   immediates). And the 0.5 lerp is 0x004d0f08 - a THIRD reader,
   after the crash rider-speed damping and the wall rebound
   fraction.

9.47.4.  leanPrev, and why it always read zero

   racer+0x2e21 was named unk2e21 in src/ with the note "reads 0.0 in
   every sample". It is leanPrev, and the note explains itself once
   this function is read: ONLY THE AI PATH WRITES IT, and every live
   capture this project has taken watched racer 1, the player.

   THAT IS A CLEAN EXAMPLE OF A NEGATIVE OBSERVATION BEING ABOUT
   METHOD RATHER THAN CODE - the fourth in this document, after
   9.18.2, 9.19.3 and 9.32.3. "Always reads zero" meant "we never
   looked at a racer that writes it". The pattern is consistent
   enough now to check first: before concluding a field is unused,
   ask which racer the samples came from.

9.47.5.  Transcribed WHOLE, with three marked holes

   racer_tick_ai in racer.c is maybe_Racer_TickAI end to end, twelve
   numbered steps in the original's order.  The two earlier slices -
   racer_ai_wall_hit and racer_ai_ground_snap - are folded into it
   and gone; racer_ai_lean_step stays a separate function because it
   is the one block with a measurement and a correction behind it
   (9.47.3).  MR_Racer gains moveDir at +0x18c, the two contact-pair
   flags, and leanPrev replacing unk2e21.

   THE AI AND THE PLAYER BUILD matOrientation BY THE SAME
